The participants' interactions realize the control arrangement. An architecture description represents selected relationships in that arrangement and the controlled system for a particular question.

An organization can precede the choice of a method, and its participants can perform work that changes the organization itself. A proposed architecture can guide a comparison. Once an authorized decision adopts it, the selected design can govern construction before the intended arrangement exists. Establish the actual arrangement from the relations that organize the identified holon at the relevant time. Where a decision itself makes a delegation effective, the applicable rule and effective act can establish that particular organizational change.

For an explanatory heuristic, use its distinctions to investigate what happens, develop an explanation or design an intervention. Select the intervention's operations and conditions on their own grounds. The expansive-learning model below, for example, helps investigate transformation of collective activity; its ideal cycle does not prescribe a universal sequence for every participant.

One Method can be performed by one person or several departments. One organization can use several Methods. One participant can observe, choose, act and reconsider the way of working; several participants can also share these contributions. Four labels on a diagram establish neither four components nor four occurrences of Work. Where a formal identity claim about a Method, its description or actual Work is needed, use the defining rules for that claim.

[C.32.MWA.PP](FPF-Spec.md#c32mwapp---construct-and-compare-method-performance-and-provision-arrangements) supplies the connection of performance and provision. [C.30.LCA:4.1](FPF-Spec.md#c30lca41---interpret-control-relations-and-the-claims-they-support) explains which control relations can support a claim about a component or the whole.

#### Library.Reference:6.5.2 - Follow the relationships that make the next action possible

**Observation must become a useful distinction for choice.** A queue alone does not tell the participants whether delay comes from insufficient capacity, unready inputs or a missing decision right. Interpretation connects observations with the situation and consequences of possible actions. An adequate familiar judgement can suffice; an explicit prediction, causal investigation or additional observation is needed when the subject method and question require it.

**Choice depends on the result and acceptable losses.** Increasing output, keeping a promise, preserving quality and developing a person's ability can call for different actions. A criterion can change, but changing it is a decision with affected recipients and authority of its own. Consent to participate, authority over shared capacity and permission to change a commitment retain their organizational meaning when described through control.

**The selected action must be obtainable.** A recommendation, permission, commitment and completed action contribute differently. Correct information can arrive too late; a manager can agree with a change while lacking the required resource. A compact method often relies on existing performance, coordination and provision. A fuller source method may itself explain how to build them.

**Build a joint proposal where the undertaking is still open.** Participants can begin with a shared concern, a provisional direction or an existing goal. Develop the proposal from the actual work: a contribution, its grounds and the support it needs. The return can change the goal or its local interpretation, the proposed way, the provision or the participants. For a commission to prepare a proposal, identify the affected contributions and return the proposal to its authorized recipient. When entrusted with arranging the change, obtain the decisions and communication it requires through the people, services or representatives entitled to act or receive the information. The undertaking becomes supportable when the required agreement, authority and means are in place.

**Distinguish what is returning.** Before execution, participants examine a proposal's grounds, compatibility and acceptability. Their responses can regulate the work of constructing that proposal; they are not observations of the proposed operation's results. Once the operation runs, its actual consequences can inform a change to it or its conditions. A coach can also use the participant's own reasoning and attempts to develop their ability. These returns concern different subjects and require different grounds, even when one conversation serves all three.

**Consequences return to the premise they can change.** They may support a new account of the situation, a different current action, a revised choice rule, changed means or allocation, development of a performer, or reconsideration of a goal. A good answer does not establish all these results. Retaining an adequate action can also be the useful outcome of feedback.

When recurrence threatens the required result and its cause is unresolved, investigate what reproduces the departure. Its frequency alone does not identify a cause or select a remedy. Connect the observation to a discriminating explanation, an available change and a later observation that can test its effect. The change may concern the current action, a choice rule or the conditions that let participants act together. Adequate current response can continue while that inquiry proceeds. When a setting rule itself no longer fits the result, environment or means, reconsider the rule and the grounds for choosing its replacement.

**Timing and joint action connect the relationships.** Observation frequency, decision time, action duration and the goal's horizon differ. Several locally permissible choices can together exceed a shared resource. The useful repair changes the information, choice, authority, allocation or work on which the joint result depends. C.43:4.1–4.6 develops these general dependencies.

Name the relation that orders what the working account calls *levels*. For composition, identify the whole and how its parts work together. For control, identify which decision can change a reference, limit or rule used by another controller. A comparison of model abstraction asks what a model allows and what a more concrete model fixes for the same inquiry. A field can supply a rule for one constituent or for a whole organization. Library.Reference:6.2 also distinguishes authority, timing and functional grouping. One person or module can supply several functions, and one function can require several participants.

To establish control of a controller, identify who or what sets a reference, limit or mode, how the setting reaches the controller, and which action it governs. A dispatcher can change a setpoint on a fixed schedule while the local controller responds to its own sensor. To establish feedback at the dispatcher, also trace an observation of the controlled operation back to the dispatcher and show how it can affect a later setting. If the conclusion concerns organizational authority, identify who may make that change.

Consider two constructed arrangements. In the first, a dispatcher receives a reliable queue state, selects the next job by an adequate urgency rule, assigns a capable performer and receives completion information. The goal, rule, capacity and authority remain adequate over the relevant interval. This observation–choice–action–return relationship is sufficient for selecting the next job.

In the second, the same dispatcher also has responsibility and authority to revise the urgency rule while preserving commitments. From accumulated outcomes and acceptable losses, the dispatcher compares rules, introduces a selected rule and observes whether later decisions improve. The revised rule guides job selection, and the results of job selection inform its next revision. One person supplies both relationships. By contrast, updating a counter or threshold according to an already fixed rule can remain part of one adaptive controller. The additional relationship follows from what is selected, changed and assessed.

Distribution concerns participation in these relationships. Teams can coordinate shared capacity through effective agreements without a common superior; several managers can pass along one decision without independently revising its grounds. Describe which rights, interactions and means an organizational change alters.

Adaptation and learning likewise need a subject. An updated state estimate, retained AI memory, trained model weights, a distinction acquired by a person and revised organizational norms are different results. A single episode can contribute to several when their relationships and evidence are preserved. C.43:4.8 supplies development of the method and its provision; C.40.JD connects development of the method and performer. Use these contributions when their problems arise.

**Connect learning to subsequent use.** For continuing joint work, ask what must remain obtainable after the present encounter. The result may depend on a person's ability to make a distinction, an explanation another practitioner can recover, or an interaction through which specialists combine different contributions. Provide the needed connection and observe its use. Joint work can rely on complementary abilities; developing the same ability in every participant is an additional goal.

When participants seek to improve how they learn together, identify the interaction they can change and who can make the change effective. A facilitator can improve questioning within an agreed encounter. Changing access to information, protected practice time or rights over shared decisions requires the corresponding participants and authority. Later work must show whether the changed condition lets the needed contribution reach its use.

#### Library.Reference:6.5.3 - Preserve the different family connections

The field is organized here by the relationship that a prescription helps build or change, or an explanatory model helps understand. Maintaining work, testing a process change, regulating flow, acting in changing interaction, developing ability and transforming collective practice overlap. A method can address several of these difficulties. The following connections explain significant variants; they are not a closed classification of cycle names.

##### Library.Reference:6.5.3.1 - Process performance, an improvement test and sustained use

In the [Model for Improvement](https://www.ihi.org/library/model-for-improvement), an improvement aim, useful measures and an idea for change guide PDSA. Performers predict the result of a bounded test, conduct it, compare observations with the prediction and select the next move. The test supplies grounds for a decision. Sustained use of the change requires everyday work to be organized. [IHI distinguishes testing from implementation](https://www.ihi.org/library/model-for-improvement/implementing-changes), including training, documentation and supporting conditions.

**Choose what the next test must establish.** An early PDSA can answer a small question about feasibility or the participants' response. A claim that the change caused an improvement needs observations and a comparison that can distinguish the relevant alternatives; the number of completed cycles does not supply that basis. Implementation establishes continuing use through the actual workflow, responsibilities and support. It can itself require tests. When [spreading a change](https://www.ihi.org/library/model-for-improvement/spreading-changes), the receiving participants must establish how it fits and works in their conditions. Knowledge from a trial, sustained practice and the organization's ability to conduct further improvement are different results.

**Connect the change with its implementation.** IHI's [Core Components Guide](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/health-services/articles/10.3389/frhs.2026.1751580/full) distinguishes a *content theory*, explaining why selected changes should improve the system, from an *execution theory*, explaining how the initiative will make their use obtainable. Develop these together with people who perform and receive the work: connect the problem, aim and proposed changes with time, data, learning, cooperation and responsibility. Observe both the system's results and the conduct of the initiative. Revisit their connections as understanding changes. The components support an iterative design; their numbered presentation does not prescribe a linear sequence.

**Return evidence to the contribution it can change.** [A.3.1.MI:4.5](FPF-Spec.md#a31mi45---explain-a-changed-result-before-selecting-an-adaptation) supplies the fuller comparison when the next action depends on distinguishing delivery, mechanism and context. If the intended action did not occur, inspect access, allocation, capability and support before rejecting its proposed mechanism. If it occurred but the expected consequence did not, examine that mechanism, the relevant conditions and competing explanations. A useful result obtained through additional help can justify retaining that help while leaving the original explanation unresolved. Distinguish improvement in the service, the intended performer's acquired ability and the organization's ability to obtain the work. Meetings, coaching hours and completed tests can describe activity; later performance under the required conditions is needed for the corresponding ability claim.

Use [IS.3](DPFs/IMPLEMENTATION-FACILITATION-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#is3---construct-and-adapt-a-support-strategy-with-its-recipients) when the missing contribution is how the practice becomes usable. Use a result already in hand when it answers the present need under current conditions and the recipient can act on it. If a fresh result is needed, check whether an existing service can supply it in time. If access or another prerequisite for use is missing, check whether restoring it would suffice. An instruction, record or observation may already show how the required contribution reaches its user. If a consequential gap remains, obtain the missing evidence. Work through a representative case together when the gap calls for joint interpretation, design or agreement. When the aim is for the recipient to learn the operation, arrange an attempt and feedback on the recipient's work. A different introduction can preserve the target way. A change to its decisive operation or criterion returns to that way's owner. [OPS.22](DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/OPERATIONS-MANAGEMENT-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#ops22---interpret-operating-performance-and-decide-the-response) supplies interpretation of actual operating results; [OCE.13](DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/ORGANIZATION-CHANGE-ENGINEERING-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#oce13---observe-and-compare-organization-change-consequences) compares consequences when the organization arrangement changes, including work transferred to other participants. Use their sufficient existing results directly.

**Connect continuing improvement to organizational decisions.** [Quality as an Organizational Strategy](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/health-services/articles/10.3389/frhs.2026.1726688/full) connects the need served and the organization's purpose with its interdependent processes, measures, information and improvement work. Information reaches distinct uses: an immediate response to a problem, feedback to an existing improvement effort, or analysis for future planning. Planning connects strategic objectives to the processes that need change and produces both operating plans and a resourced improvement portfolio. Leaders sponsor the work, help remove obstacles and use its results to learn about the organization. Existing improvement projects can provide the starting point while the other connections develop. A process map makes dependencies discussable; its arrangement on the page does not establish decision rights.

[STR.12](DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/STRATEGY-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#str12---observe-signals-to-continue-or-reconsider-strategic-direction-and-commitments) connects a qualified signal to the premise and authorized response it can change. Use [STR.16](DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/STRATEGY-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#str16---construct-a-feasible-portfolio-with-shared-or-branch-specific-support) when several worthwhile undertakings and their support still need a feasible combination. Separate one undertaking's need for another's result from competition for the same person or resource. Obtain the required contributions and commitments, then observe whether they let the receiving work proceed. Experience can warrant revising an initiative, its support, the portfolio or the strategic direction. Each revision needs the people authorized to decide it and those who can perform the change. These relationships can be distributed among participants or supplied by the same people.

A local test with adequate provision can finish its bounded question without constructing a new organization-wide programme. Retain a sufficient current method and arrangement. Expand the inquiry or arrangement when the needed evidence, continuing work, shared capacity or decision right is missing or changes. Close the present question at its supported scope while preserving the later observation or dependency that can reopen it.

Recover the selected formulation of PDCA or PDSA. The [Deming Institute's PDSA account](https://deming.org/explore/pdsa/) emphasizes study of a prediction and permits reconsidering theory and goals. [ASQ's PDCA](https://asq.org/quality-resources/pdca-cycle) also includes analysis and learning in Check. Treating every PDCA as compliance checking would lose that variant. An experiment can inform a goal change; the actual organization must still provide the decision right.

Imai's SDCA/PDCA distinction makes the relationship between maintaining and changing a standard explicit. Performers sustain work under the current standard and discover departures; improvement supplies grounds to revise that standard. Further work uses the revision, and its results inform the next reconsideration. One process owner can participate in both relationships. The basis is *Gemba Kaizen*, second edition, chapter 1, and the [Kaizen Institute's account of standardization and improvement](https://kaizen.com/insights/standardize-improve-standards/). Maintaining an adequate way need not carry the burden of another improvement investigation.

[DMAIC](https://asq.org/quality-resources/dmaic) adds useful measurement and investigation of causes before changing the process. Its Control phase organizes sustained results and response to later departures. ASQ distinguishes DMADV where designing a new process is required. [A3](https://info.lean.org/hubfs/ebook%20files/How-to-use-A3-PDF-final.pdf), particularly pages 3–9 of the LEI account, connects problem investigation with participants' examination of the reasoning, agreement and responsibility for action. The sheet supports those interactions. These variants explain how a PDSA test can contribute inside a more developed undertaking.

**Distinguish the difficulty before choosing the response.** Begin with the result, population, period and requirement that matter to the recipient. For a defective item, apply the relevant acceptance or protective rule. A statistical signal concerns departure from an established process behavior. An unreliable measurement leaves the attributed property uncertain. A missed operation raises a question about actual execution and its conditions. These difficulties can coexist. [OPS.18](DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/OPERATIONS-MANAGEMENT-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#ops18---control-operating-quality-and-reliability) connects each evidence question to its available action and authority. Act on a known requirement violation when its response is already warranted, while obtaining any diagnosis still needed for prevention. A stable process can repeatedly produce unacceptable output; statistical stability alone does not settle acceptance or process capability.

**Establish what the observations represent.** Recover the unit or event, inclusion rule, time order, measuring procedure and relevant operating conditions. [OPS.15](DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/OPERATIONS-MANAGEMENT-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#ops15---build-a-decision-specific-operating-account) supplies the population and event account; [C.16:5.1–5.4](FPF-Spec.md#c1651---name-the-measurand-and-measurement-subject) connects an indication to the intended property, calibration and uncertainty. Repeated readings of one unchanged item can expose measurement variation; they do not sample the variation among produced items. Before adjusting the process, test whether a change of gauge, operator or recording rule contributed to the apparent change. If the rule changed, apply both rules to the same retained observations where possible. If a new gauge gives a different result on a later batch, use an established comparison of the gauges under the relevant conditions, or compare them on the same stable specimen when needed. Both the measurement and the process may have changed. [NIST's Gauge R&R guidance](https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/handbook/mpc/section4/mpc41.htm) identifies repeatability, reproducibility, stability and bias as distinct measurement questions. More readings under the same shared bias leave that bias unresolved.

For monitoring, establish the baseline and a suitable observation and response rule, then compare later behavior with that basis. [NIST distinguishes baseline establishment from subsequent monitoring](https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/handbook/pmc/section1/pmc12.htm). Dependence, changing case mix or grouping can change the interpretation and the monitor needed. A new baseline requires grounds for the changed reference; an unfavorable observation alone is no reason to erase the departure. Use the existing qualified monitor or acceptance plan when it already answers the question.

**Connect the investigation to the explanation or change required.** DMAIC connects a defined problem to usable measurements, an account of causes, a proposed remedy and continuing control. Return between these contributions when a finding changes the question: a recording error can invalidate the apparent defect pattern; a failed remedy can challenge the proposed mechanism. [C.28.CM](FPF-Spec.md#c28cm---construct-and-challenge-a-causal-model) supplies the comparison of material causal explanations, including measurement and selection. Choose another observation for what it could reveal about the mechanism or the consequences of a remedy. If the retained explanations already support the same sufficient protection, use it with its stated limits. A commission to establish the cause of the defect still calls for an account of how it arose. A commission to prevent recurrence calls for a way to keep the process effective under the relevant conditions. Either may require distinguishing mechanisms that support the same immediate protection.

**Compare interventions through their consequences.** Finding that an input affects an output does not yet establish how much changing that input would improve ordinary production. For an output Y that depends linearly on input X, write `Y = a + bX + e`: a is an offset, b the sensitivity and e the remaining influence. With independent X and e and finite variances, `Var(Y) = b² Var(X) + Var(e)`. A suitable experiment can estimate the effect b, while the ordinary distribution of X needs its own basis; experimenter-selected levels need not reproduce it. This is a useful distinction in [Panahi, Steiner and de Mast's verification study](https://doi.org/10.1080/08982112.2023.2253303), §§2–3.1. Compare feasible changes to the input distribution, sensitivity, centering or process design against the required output and their full consequences. A statistically significant effect or a large explained share alone does not select the remedy.

Use [C.28.MR](FPF-Spec.md#c28mr---derive-an-intervention-consequence-by-mechanism-replacement) when the consequence still needs to be derived from a model. An applicable estimate or earlier derivation can already answer a request to compare the interventions. For a new derivation, identify the mechanism replaced and the other mechanisms and input conditions that still apply. Follow those relations to the result the question needs. They may establish a direction of change, a bound or a quantitative effect. A common cause of X and Y can leave output variation after X is fixed; their squared correlation alone does not quantify the reduction from that intervention. Carry model uncertainty and uncertainty in estimated quantities into the comparison when they can change the decision. [MMP.13](DPFs/Foundational%20Thinking%20DPF%20Suite/MATHEMATICAL-MODELING-PRACTICE-DPF.md#mmp13---infer-unknowns-statistically-under-a-stated-observation-model) supplies inference under the actual observation law. Process requirements can concern tails, centering or asymmetric consequences; a smaller variance is useful only through the result it improves.

**Select observations for the distinction needed.** A study aimed at separating causes can deliberately choose contrasting cases. The [2025 review of leveraged sample selection](https://doi.org/10.1080/08982112.2024.2410012) explains how parts near opposite ends of a known baseline can help investigate variation. That choice needs a justified baseline, an appropriate study and analysis that account for selection. Very extreme cases may belong to another failure mechanism. Such an investigation answers a different question from estimating the ordinary frequency of defects. [MMP.16](DPFs/Foundational%20Thinking%20DPF%20Suite/MATHEMATICAL-MODELING-PRACTICE-DPF.md#mmp16---design-observations-to-separate-mathematical-model-alternatives) connects the alternatives, obtainable records and receiving decision. Use existing information when it is sufficient; obtain a new study when its attainable contribution warrants its cost and delay.

**A conditional calculation can reject an insufficient remedy.** Suppose a centered, stable process is modeled by `Y = 10 + 0.5X + e`, with independent normal X and e, means zero, `Var(X) = 4` and `Var(e) = 1`. The measurement basis and execution are assumed adequate. Individual output must lie between 8 and 12, and the process objective is at most 2% outside that interval. Under these assumptions:

| Compared condition | Output variance | Modeled fraction outside 8–12 |
| --- | ---: | ---: |
| Current process | 2 | 15.73% |
| Reduce Var(X) from 4 to 1; retain sensitivity 0.5 | 1.25 | 7.36% |
| Reduce sensitivity from 0.5 to 0.25; retain Var(X) = 4 | 1.25 | 7.36% |
| Hold X at zero; retain the residual mechanism | 1 | 4.55% |

The alternatives leave the stated centering and residual distribution unchanged. Even ideal control of X misses the 2% objective. To meet the 2% objective, obtain another effective change, such as reducing residual variation. If the requirement itself is reconsidered, the authorized decision changes the target of the comparison. The calculation can end the search for a sufficient X-only remedy while containment continues. It does not choose among the remaining changes or establish that the assumed model describes an actual process. A measured improvement would still need its own observations and comparison.

**Return the chosen change to sustainable work.** DMAIC Control and SDCA connect the selected way to actual performance, observation and a response to departure. Make the operating method and required inputs obtainable, assign capable performers, and provide the time, means and rights needed to act. State who can contain output, restore operation, authorize restart or revise the standard. Observe whether the changed practice continues and whether the required result follows. [OPS.16](DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/OPERATIONS-MANAGEMENT-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#ops16---decide-whether-and-how-to-change-an-operating-method) compares a reusable method change with alternatives; OPS.18 supplies containment and restart. Use the implementation connection above when the missing contribution is how the practice becomes usable.

When a departure from a standard leaves the next response unresolved, distinguish the missing contribution. Restore missing tools, access or time when these prevented performance. If the difficulty concerns an operation the person has not yet mastered, arrange an attempt, usable instruction, correction and follow-up. The [TWI Institute's account of Job Instruction](https://www.twi-institute.com/twi-job-instruction-removes-barriers/) connects preparation and demonstration with trying the job and subsequent help; [HCD.9](DPFs/Human%20Capability%20Engineering%20DPF%20Suite/HUMAN-CAPABILITY-DEVELOPMENT-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#hcd9---perform-deliberate-practice-with-feedback) supplies the feedback and retry under stated support conditions. When adequate performance under the intended conditions still fails the requirement, reconsider the way and its causal grounds. Statistical process capability and a person's ability to perform the work are different claims. Retain a useful reference, colleague or other support when it belongs to the intended work; successful assisted performance need not imply independent ability.

Maintaining a sufficient standard and its provision is an ordinary completion of this control question. A local correction can finish without launching a new DMAIC project. Reopen the affected relation when measurement, process behavior, requirements, execution or provision changes enough to alter the warranted action. A missing decision right or shared resource returns to the organizational connections above; another repetition of the local cycle cannot supply it.

##### Library.Reference:6.5.3.2 - TOC: current flow, its settings and its transformation

In the Buffer Management account of [Cox III and Lynn H. Boyd, §3](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7476509/), buffer state guides a current response to a threat to flow. Recurrent states can also lead to resizing the buffer, changing conditions of later response. [Five Focusing Steps](https://www.tocinstitute.org/five-focusing-steps.html) relates the result of the whole system to its constraint: identify it, exploit its available capability, subordinate other decisions, elevate capability when needed and return to the focus when the constraint changes.

In the same Cox–Boyd account, Change Question Sequence connects undesirable effects and their causes with designing a change, involving participants, implementing it and sustaining the result. These contributions can connect as follows: current regulation reveals a problem; inquiry identifies the constraint or rule to change; implementation establishes new conditions for current work.

That is one possible arrangement for a stated problem. Membership in TOC does not impose four levels of reaction, tuning, focusing and transformation. Buffer resizing can be explained within one adaptive controller, or its management can be distinguished when its criteria, consequences and rights need separate attention. Cox–Boyd supplies one POOGI line, not the canonical structure of every TOC practice.

**Connect current response to changed conditions.** In [Cox III's PASS execution account for patient flow, §5, steps 8–10](https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/abs/10.1080/20476965.2019.1646105), staff respond to buffer warnings and record the causes of threatened flow. Recurring causes inform a selected change whose effect is then monitored. Significant environmental change can require reconsidering the system through the thinking processes and focusing steps. The connection is observation → explanation → change → subsequent performance. A buffer warning supports a current response; a recorded recurrence supports investigation; neither alone establishes which condition to change. If the present response restores adequate flow and no consequential recurring cause remains unresolved, another transformation is unnecessary for that departure. If an input, allocation or rule repeatedly recreates the departure, select a change to that condition and make its implementation and subsequent assessment possible.

**Reconnect flow across organizations.** A locally improved operation can still receive unsuitable inputs or pass work into an unavailable downstream service. The controlled whole then depends on the links between participants as well as their local performance. [Cox III and Mabin's healthcare supply-chain proposal, §§4–5.3](https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/abs/10.1080/20476965.2025.2460632) addresses local units and their reconnection: practices develop their own flow, then work with partners on urgency, scheduling, roles and the conditions of transfer. Owners and managers participate in choosing and adopting these arrangements. The proposed decomposition into local problems and subsequent linking of organizations (stages I and III) were not fully implemented, and the described referral route excludes emergency-department entry. The paper supplies a design and implementation approach within those limits, not evidence of a completed transformation of every link.

An effective agreement connects the receiving work to participants who can supply the needed information, capacity and action on the agreed terms. A beneficial proposal or a shared aim does not confer authority over another organization's resources. Examine the effects on each participant and the conditions under which they can agree. If their commitments remain incompatible, return to the commitments or the arrangement that could reconcile them. Local gains leave this dependency unresolved until the participants can coordinate the required work. Decomposing a system into practices, allocating authority and coordinating across practices describe different relationships; none by itself establishes a hierarchy of controllers.

**Connect operating rules, tactical settings and strategic direction.** The Demand Driven Institute's [Demand Driven Material Requirements Planning (DDMRP)](https://www.demanddriveninstitute.com/ddmrp) includes tactical adaptation; its [Demand Driven Operating Model (DDOM)](https://www.demanddriveninstitute.com/demand-driven-operating-model) connects order generation, scheduling and execution. In the [Demand Driven Adaptive Enterprise (DDAE)](https://www.demanddriveninstitute.com/demand-driven-adaptive-enterprise-m), [Demand Driven S&OP](https://www.demanddriveninstitute.com/demand-driven-s-op) uses past and projected operations together with strategic direction to revise configuration. It returns capability projections and recommendations to strategic leadership. [Adaptive S&OP](https://www.demanddriveninstitute.com/adaptive-s-op) can revise direction and business assumptions; the resulting requirements guide tactical configuration and operation. Beginning this bidirectional adaptation requires at least a partly implemented operating model, capable participants and a defined direction. These are the Institute's current methodological formulations, not comparative evidence that DDAE is preferable in every setting.

Executing a formula that updates a buffer parameter can realize an already prescribed operating rule. Assessing that rule's suitability is a further relationship: use outcomes and a criterion to choose whether to retain or revise the rule, make an authorized change effective when needed, and assess later performance. The same participant can supply both relationships. Explain the additional relationship when it matters to the decision; the names operational, tactical and strategic do not establish three organizational ranks. DDAE is a related synthesis with its own conditions, not a definition of every POOGI practice.

**Let causal and dynamic accounts revise each other.** [Mabin and Cavana's combined TOC/qualitative system dynamics framework](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1002/sdr.1768) connects stakeholder concerns and behaviour over time with causal-loop modelling, TOC investigation of effects, assumptions and conflicts, and revision of the proposed intervention. Insights return to the dynamic account; goal and causal accounts can also be revised as understanding develops. Select this inquiry when feedback, delayed effects or interactions between participants could change the proposed intervention. A familiar adequately explained operating choice need not carry the whole investigation.

Use necessary-condition reasoning to identify prerequisites and sufficient-cause reasoning to examine how conditions together would produce an effect. Dynamic inquiry examines feedback and delays over time. The qualitative TOC trees and signed feedback links do not by themselves supply estimated effect sizes, delays or an established future trajectory. The paper's revised behaviour-over-time curves are hypothetical, and implementation and organizational learning were not demonstrated in its teaching exercise. Use the combined account to generate and examine a proposed change; obtain observations of implementation and effects before claiming that the change worked.

##### Library.Reference:6.5.3.3 - Boyd: orientation and coordinated initiative

In Boyd's original OODA, orientation shapes what a participant notices and how the participant acts. Implicit guidance permits action without a separate explicit decision at every step. New information, consequences of action, analysis and synthesis can change orientation. An explicit decision can be a hypothesis and action its test. Recover these relationships from the full diagram and explanation rather than a four-arrow mnemonic.

The organizational account connects common understanding and intent with participants' initiative. They choose actions in local conditions; information about what happens can refine direction and interaction. In *Organic Design*, Boyd develops his own distinction between command/control and appreciation/leadership. Understanding and assessment connect to directing joint activity, with observation conducted in a way that does not obstruct operations.

FPF's general control account can explain these functional relationships while preserving Boyd's particular meanings of control and leadership. Their correspondence does not establish a common organizational hierarchy for every OODA use. The basis is the [original briefings in Air University's *A Discourse on Winning and Losing*](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/GOVPUB-D301-PURL-gpo94257/pdf/GOVPUB-D301-PURL-gpo94257.pdf), *Organic Design* at printed pages 218–253, especially 248–253, and the complete *Essence of Winning and Losing*, including the implicit connections.

**Distinguish freedom in execution from responsibility for an operation.** A participant may choose how to carry out a task while the purpose, shared allocations and decisions about other participants remain elsewhere. Entrusting an operation adds decisions about how its contributions will be planned, made available, performed and assessed. Recover which of those decisions the recipient may actually make and which commitments still require another owner. This applies the goal, mandate and exchange distinctions in [C.32.LCA:4.2–4.8](FPF-Spec.md#c32lca---construct-and-compare-a-control-architecture).

The US Air Force's [AFDP 3-0.1, *Command and Control*, 22 January 2025, pp. 4–12](https://www.doctrine.af.mil/Portals/61/documents/AFDP_3-0_1/AFDP3-0.1CommandandControl.pdf) develops a current doctrinal distinction alongside its decision cycle: decentralized execution permits initiative within intent, while distributed control delegates authority to conduct the planning, preparation, execution and assessment of assigned operations. The receiving organization needs the relevant capability and capacity. Distribution depends on the operation and communication conditions; centralized control remains a conditional alternative. Its military authority structure is particular to that source.

For a project, construct the allocation through C.32.LCA. A service team allowed to reorder its jobs may still need the service owner's decision to change a promised result or obtain another team's capacity. A wider mandate is useful only if the team can obtain the information, means and cooperation required to use it. Establish any needed reservation or agreement before relying on it. When communication may fail, make the permitted continuation and the observation that ends it usable in advance; on return, reconcile changed commitments and pending actions. If one existing decision point already has the needed information, rights and timely capacity, retain that arrangement. A missing input, unavailable receiver or conflicting commitment selects the relation to repair; another OODA repetition alone cannot supply it.

##### Library.Reference:6.5.3.4 - Kata: direction, learner development and help for the coach

Rother's Improvement Kata connects a desired direction with a current process, a near target condition and successive observations or experiments. Use [E.23:4.4.1](FPF-Spec.md#e23441---improve-a-work-process-through-target-conditions-and-experiments) for the process-improvement way: it explains how to derive the next target from actual work, distinguish an obstacle from a preferred solution, choose among observation, exploratory change and hypothesis testing, and use the result to determine the next move. Its contract-entry case follows that reasoning through a changed condition. A target condition specifies a desired operating state and outcome within a period; it is not a list of the solutions presumed necessary to reach it.

The learner's ability to perform that reasoning is a second subject. [HCD.9:4.10](DPFs/Human%20Capability%20Engineering%20DPF%20Suite/HUMAN-CAPABILITY-DEVELOPMENT-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#hcd9410---coach-scientific-reasoning-during-process-improvement) supplies coached practice of the particular actions: expose the learner's reasoning, locate a missing or unsupported connection, provide usable help and inspect the learner's response. The second coach applies the same attempt-and-feedback discipline to the coach's actual conversation. An improved process, a corrected learner inference and a better coaching action therefore have distinct grounds, although the same episode can supply evidence for each.

A prepared practitioner can use the process-improvement way without adding a novice coaching programme. When development of the reasoning is intended, select a manageable process and enough coaching for the learner's current difficulty. A conversation can finish when a useful next step and expected learning are clear, including a return to observation.

Starter routines give learners a way to practise scientific thinking and novice coaches a way to learn how to observe and guide it. Use the prescribed routine while acquiring the thinking or coaching pattern. Once the participant has internalized that pattern, they can retain a useful routine or adapt its form when doing so better supports the work. Preserve the learner's prediction, observation of what happened and use of the difference in the next step.

Choose the next practice episode from the learner's actual attempt, the help received and the conditions in which the ability will be needed. [HCD.9 and HCD.10](DPFs/Human%20Capability%20Engineering%20DPF%20Suite/HUMAN-CAPABILITY-DEVELOPMENT-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md) supply this connection: HCD.9 makes the attempt and use of feedback observable; HCD.10 helps choose whether to keep the present practice or change the task or support, and which observation is needed to retain, revise or reverse that choice. Changing task conditions or help can preserve the same starter routine. Keep or restore support when the task needs it; later work may legitimately include references, tools or a coach. A harder subject problem may require additional knowledge, means or authority. When the person can already perform the required work with its permitted support, use that arrangement and close the bounded practice question.

Organizational deployment adds another relationship: a target condition at one organizational level becomes direction for the next. A manager can be a learner in relation to one coach and a coach to others. Rother usually combines management and coaching roles, while allowing exceptions; the second coach can be a peer or staff specialist. These relationships therefore cannot be recovered from a single ladder of job titles. The distinction between large and small PDCA concerns progress toward a target condition and individual experiments, a different relationship again.

**Coordinate experiments that depend on other participants.** In Rother and Aulinger's *Toyota Kata Culture* (2017), chapter 4, short group coaching cycles expose obstacles at interfaces while individual coaching develops each learner's way of thinking. The group examines target and actual conditions, the obstacle being addressed, the next step and its expected result, and when that result can be observed. Reflection on the preceding experiment uses its expectation and actual result. Participants can affect one another's work without reporting to one another.

A learner can request help, the coach can notice the need, or another team can report a problem. Determine what contribution is missing. An organizational obstacle may require resources, cooperation or a decision from another participant. An unresolved technical cause may require specialist help or further observation. Examine the next experiment's consequences for other work. Involve the participants who can supply what is missing or whose work the experiment would change. Use direct contact where it is available; a coach can mediate a connection that the learner cannot make.

In the book's constructed case, Roger needs to stop machining for changeover experiments toward a 54-minute target condition. He needs four hours of buffer stock before stopping. Steve involves Thomas because Roger cannot directly obtain that contribution in the depicted arrangement. Asked what prevents producing the stock, Thomas explains that the necessary raw material is about to arrive and expects the buffer to be built by the end of the week. The dialogue identifies a prerequisite and the provider's own dependency. In applying this arrangement, establish that the stock and timing fit before relying on the stoppage. [LDR.1](DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/LEADERSHIP-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md) helps participants conduct that conversation. When commitments need reconciling, they can use LDR.2 to reach agreement and establish what each has accepted.

A different case starts when Steve notices Robert's difficulty. Robert reports a delay every third machine cycle and unsatisfactory results from root-cause analysis. Steve proposes visiting the process together after the group cycle to choose the next step from what they observe.

The illustrated summary board distinguishes a missing coaching-cycle card from a red card indicating delay or a need for help. Ask what prevented the work and whether the pair can handle it locally. The arrangement permits more frequent coaching at the affected point and further help when that is insufficient. Its schedule and display can vary with the work. Follow the selected contribution into the experiment and return its consequences to the target and other participants' work.

**Carry the experiment's facts into the wider dialogue.** Chapter 3 shows Nancy's difficulty in relying on Steve's recollection of his learners' storyboards. Visiting the processes and boards can provide the basis. Where that is impractical, the book illustrates summary cards prepared immediately after individual coaching cycles from the learners' experimenting records. They carry target and actual conditions, learning, the obstacle addressed, and the next step with its expectation. Another means of carrying those facts can serve the same purpose.

Nancy first examines the relevant learners' experiments, then Steve's own obstacle and experiment. Steve has been trying to make production time available for Roger's experiments and learns that the scheduler supplies the best information about suitable times.

**Develop the ability to sustain the arrangement.** A second coach observes a coaching cycle and gives focused feedback for the next attempt; [HCD.9:4.10](DPFs/Human%20Capability%20Engineering%20DPF%20Suite/HUMAN-CAPABILITY-DEVELOPMENT-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#hcd9410---coach-scientific-reasoning-during-process-improvement) explains this practice. The book's Conclusion applies Improvement Kata to deployment itself: examine the actual practice, identify the next obstacle to an expansion that can be supported, try a change and use its result. Choose the scope of expansion from the coaching capability that can be provided.

**Begin when experienced internal coaches are absent.** The *Practice Guide* (2018), chapter 3, gives two ways to start. An experienced external coach can first coach managers as Improvement Kata learners; they subsequently learn to coach others. Alternatively, three participants can practise through temporary rotation of learner, coach and observing second-coach roles. With separate focus processes, each person works as learner on their own process and as coach and observer for the others; the group performs all three rotations each day. With one shared process, the participants rotate roles periodically; the book illustrates a change after ten coaching cycles. These arrangements require different process access and amounts of shared practice time. Choose the arrangement whose practice can be sustained.

At the start the coaching roles may exist in name only. Use the observed interactions and feedback in HCD.9:4.10 to develop and assess what each participant can perform; obtain qualified help where the group cannot supply the judgement it needs. The book presents rotation as a temporary way to acquire experience, typically over six to eight weeks of practice. That illustrative period can guide preparation; actual capability determines which coaching work participants can undertake.

[Aulinger's *Coaching-Lerngruppen*, version 3.0, 2026](https://www.verbesserungskata.de/app/download/5790421637/Aufbau%2Beiner%2BKata-Organisation%2B3.0.pdf), printed pp. 7–17, proposes one way to prepare coaches: four participants rotate through learner, coach and observing second-coach roles, then give feedback on the coaching. A coordination group helps provide practice time and tools, resolve obstructing rules and connect the groups. Initially short process experiments let participants observe consequences and try a correction. This is an author's organizational variant, not evidence that every Kata use needs a group or that rotation itself qualifies its feedback.

Use [HCD.20](DPFs/Human%20Capability%20Engineering%20DPF%20Suite/HUMAN-CAPABILITY-DEVELOPMENT-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#hcd20---compose-and-compare-arrangements-for-individual-and-group-learning) to compare the group's needed contributions with sufficient individual practice. Preserve each person's coaching attempt and count the time of participants and helpers, including preparation and correction. Use [HCD.7](DPFs/Human%20Capability%20Engineering%20DPF%20Suite/HUMAN-CAPABILITY-DEVELOPMENT-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#hcd7---arrange-providers-access-tools-and-ai-support-for-human-capability-development) to establish who can provide the required judgement and support. A group of novices may need qualified outside help. If a conversation stalls, use [HCD.26](DPFs/Human%20Capability%20Engineering%20DPF%20Suite/HUMAN-CAPABILITY-DEVELOPMENT-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#hcd26---regulate-shared-learning) to identify the missing contribution, choose a feasible intervention and inspect its use. An unavailable instrument or decision right returns to its provider or owner. When a prepared coach and supported individual practice supply the required development, retain them. Attendance, role rotation and conversation counts answer different questions from demonstrated coaching ability.

For the direction, deployment and coaching relationships, the basis is the [*Improvement Kata Handbook*, v32.0](https://community.obeya-association.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/2024/08/Toyota-Kata_Full_IK_Handbook_v32.0_compressed.pdf), PDF pages 129–148, the diagram at 135, and pages 479–484.

The process and coaching operations above are developed from Mike Rother's *The Toyota Kata Practice Guide* (2018 electronic edition), especially Chapters 5–9 and 10–12, with its operational figures and practice forms. Rother's [Starter Kata explanation](https://public.websites.umich.edu/~jmondisa/TK/The_TK_Starter_Kata.html), [Coaching Kata explanation](https://public.websites.umich.edu/~jmondisa/TK/The_Coaching_Kata.html) and [2022 second-edition excerpt, p. vi](https://public.websites.umich.edu/~jmondisa/TK/KATA_Files/TKPG2_How_to_Use.pdf) remain separately qualified sources about starter routines and use of the guide. [Rosenthal's 2025 correction of his Kata–DMAIC comparison](https://theleanthinker.com/2025/05/06/follow-on-thoughts-what-i-missed-improvement-kata-and-dmaic/) restores development of both learner and coach and permits additional subject tools as the work requires.

##### Library.Reference:6.5.3.5 - Learning through work and changing collective activity

Learning approaches differ in what they develop and how that development reaches action. Begin with the contribution needed in subsequent work. A person may need a new distinction; practitioners may need an intelligible shared way; a group may need to investigate a problem it can act on; or the conditions of participation may prevent useful inquiry. Identify which agent can use the result of that development and what it enables the agent to do. One undertaking can require several of these contributions.

**Develop a person's use of experience.** [Kolb and Kolb's 2018 clarification](https://learningfromexperience.com/downloads/research-library/eight-important-things-to-know-about-the-experiential-learning-cycle.pdf) connects experience, reflection, conceptualization and experimentation while allowing different entry points and sequences. Facilitation, subject explanation, standards and coaching serve different learning needs. Select their contributions from the learner, subject and intended performance. A written plan for future experimentation can support reflection; its subsequent execution needs a later observation.

[HCD.15:4.2.1](DPFs/Human%20Capability%20Engineering%20DPF%20Suite/HUMAN-CAPABILITY-DEVELOPMENT-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md) supplies a useful start from successful work: recover the action and help, compare the person's explanation with a serious alternative, and retain a bounded lesson or select the next practice. HCD.9 connects feedback to another attempt; HCD.12 distinguishes use under a meaningful change from learning with a newly supplied source. Keep a sufficient explanation and action when no worthwhile learning question remains.

**Develop a question participants can investigate together.** [Senge, Hamilton and Kania](https://ssir.org/articles/entry/the_dawn_of_system_leadership) connect seeing wider relationships, examining assumptions through conversation and forming a shared direction. Their methodological account uses mapping, encounters with other practice settings and inquiry to develop these capacities. Select a tool for the relationship it helps participants examine and for the action that understanding can change.

[CB.4](DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/COMMUNITY-MANAGEMENT-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md) supplies preparation, access, facilitation and the return from a shared encounter. Where participants need to learn the reasoning, HCD.23 has them explain a consequential connection, compare its grounds and use it themselves. Where the result needed is a joint proposal, §6.5.3.8 returns its unresolved contributions and commitments to LDR and GOALS. An adequate existing answer can instead finish the inquiry.

**Make practical knowledge usable beyond its first holder.** The [Nonaka–Toyama–Konno account of SECI and ba](https://api.pageplace.de/preview/DT0400.9781847876621_A23446119/preview-9781847876621_A23446119.pdf) relates shared experience, articulation, combination of explicit accounts and learning through use. In *ba*, the shared context, participants interpret contributions and act together. Its individual, group and organizational scales describe expansion of knowledge creation. Control levels still require the relationships in §6.5.2. [Nonaka and Takeuchi's later treatment](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9753931/) keeps knowledge creation connected to practical action.

Choose what is missing in the receiving practice. A demonstration can expose the practitioner’s choice. For joint work, identify the contributions and what makes them one result. That link can be another participant’s use of a finding or an agreed rule combining independent assessments. When the order is to develop the practice itself, establish who selected that connection and why it applies to the intended uses. An explanation can make it available to an absent practitioner; subsequent use can expose a missing condition or a defective way. CB.14 develops this connection from contrasting cases through a qualified method contribution, trial, receiving use and maintenance. A ready answer can finish one case. To settle a repertoire question, establish which intended uses can be served by the available answer and its conditions. An account's availability and a person's ability to use it require different observations. KCS in §6.5.3.7 develops the corresponding connections within service work.

**Join inquiry to a real action and a learning return.** [WIAL's action-learning account](https://wial.org/action-learning/) starts with a significant problem for which a group is responsible and able to act; a coach helps it learn through inquiry and action. The [INTRAC set guide](https://www.intrac.org/app/uploads/2016/09/Action-Learning-Sets-An-INTRAC-guide-1.pdf) also supports successive individual cases: questions help the owner choose an action within their influence, and later meetings return what happened. Choose the variant from the problem owner and capacity to act.

Recover the problem with the people who have relevant experience and examine the assumptions that could change the choice. The responsible owner or group selects an action, obtains its necessary permission and means, and returns consequences to the unresolved question. CB.5 supplies a case owner's reasoned continuation and the response to contributed help. A useful conversation can finish at a clarified question when action is not yet obtainable; its learning claim must retain that limit. Use the selected source protocol where its particular question, advice and participation rules matter.

The participants can also improve the encounter itself. [Cauwelier's 2023 WIAL guidance](https://wial.org/wp-content/uploads/STOP-REMINDING-ABOUT-GROUND-RULE-1-1.pdf) moves from repeated reminders about questioning to the group's examination of why and how it communicates. This can change facilitation or a shared practice rule. The next encounter supplies an opportunity to observe that change. Reconsidering the premises that govern work is a further possible subject:

In [Argyris's *Double Loop Learning in Organizations*](https://6-30partners.com/wp-content/uploads/2015/12/Chris-Argyris-Double-Loop-Learning-in-Organisations.pdf), participants can correct action while retaining its governing premises, or investigate premises that reproduce the problem. The latter move requires discovering norms actually used, making disputed assumptions testable in joint inquiry and changing subsequent action. A declared value can differ from the value governing decisions. Defensive interaction can prevent the inquiry from changing what happens. The result must return to the relationship between the situation and action. Single- and double-loop learning distinguish what is reconsidered and made effective; they do not establish two jobs.

In expansive learning, the object of collective activity and the way of working together can change. Participants encounter contradictions, examine the existing arrangement, model and test a new one, change practice and examine consequences. Tools, rules, division of labour and interaction with neighbouring activity can change together. The aim of the transformation can itself emerge in the work.

[Engeström and Sannino, 2010, pages 6–8](https://mickhammond.blog/wp-content/uploads/2023/10/expansive-learning-.pdf) identify the ideal cycle as a conceptual heuristic. It helps investigate formation of the object and transformation of practice. A claim about a particular controller still requires showing who selects and implements which changes in that realization. The source's action/activity distinction supplies levels of analysis; it does not establish a universal seven-stage control prescription.

**Let joint inquiry change the problem being investigated.** In [Restrepo and colleagues' collaborative-learning account](https://km4djournal.org/index.php/km4dj/article/download/247/316/691), the participants, their criteria and the working question develop together. The intended users' different practices can expose a missing condition in the researcher's initial problem. Dialogue informs discovery through observation, trials or scenarios; application then tests the proposed way in the participants' circumstances and can reveal missing skills or poor fit. These relationships can recur as the collaboration develops.

[CB.14:4.2 and :5.5](DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/COMMUNITY-MANAGEMENT-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#cb1442---use-differences-to-find-a-useful-exchange) explain this contribution within joint practice development. The farming case changes a comparison of feeding practices after recovering differences in available labour and fodder. It follows that change through the professional study contribution, receiving guidance and a grant condition that can prevent the revised question from being pursued. Use CB.13 to establish how the participants can decide together. Where the new question requires changing an existing organizational relation, use OCE.14 to work out the revision and obtain the decision that puts it into effect.

The authors' [Kenyan dairy-project explanation](https://www.ditsl.org/images/articles/research/ditsl_m_fostering_change_through_collaborative_learning.pdf), p.2, describes farmers with defined roles in decisions, comparison of how they regulate milk production and quality, video proposals for action funds, and peer exchange and experiments. That realization shows how a question participants can shape reaches a trial they can perform.


[Romme and van Witteloostuijn's Endenburg case](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/236028159_Circular_organizing_and_triple_loop_learning) describes policy decisions linked across circles by a leader and elected representative, with information access and ordinary execution retained. Selecting this arrangement elsewhere requires comparison with the actual decision work and participation conditions. [Johannessen and colleagues' urban-water cases](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1002/eet.1843) connect learning with trusted exchange, capable intermediaries and institutional support, while reporting incomplete or temporary wider change. A local learning result therefore needs its own connection to any claimed continuing organizational result.

The term *triple-loop learning* requires a source-specific reading. [Tosey, Visser and Saunders](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/258171998_The_origins_and_conceptualizations_of_%27triple-loop%27_learning_A_critical_review) distinguish accounts concerned with higher governing purposes, reflection on how learning occurs, and Bateson's more radical change of learning premises. These have different objects and proposed relations. Identify the proposed change and its means; the label supplies neither an implementation nor a universal third control level.

For example, two teams may already solve their respective cases but lack an account that lets a receiving team recognize a consequential difference. CB.14 can obtain that account and try its use. If a practitioner can recover the account but cannot yet apply the distinction, select supported practice. If the teams understand the distinction but lack permission to act, identify the authority the action requires. A finding about feasibility can name the outstanding decision and its holder. When the commission includes obtaining that decision, use the authorized route to the holder. If the mandate is unclear or no one holds it, report that limit and keep the action conditional. The same eventual work result can depend on different repairs. An available explanation or direct authorized response may suffice; a recurring learning group is useful only when its continuing contribution warrants the work.

##### Library.Reference:6.5.3.6 - AI: action, retained experience and model training

In [ReAct](https://arxiv.org/html/2210.03629v3), an observation returned by external action informs further reasoning and choice in the task. In [Reflexion](https://arxiv.org/html/2303.11366v4), evaluation of an attempt and reflection produce textual memory for later attempts. Retention matters to future behaviour, but recording context does not alone establish another control level. For a question about organizing adaptation, explain how retained conclusions are selected, used and checked. For a question about answering a request, one controller with memory may be an adequate description. Actor, evaluator and reflector name functional contributions, not necessarily distinct physical participants or levels.

In [SCoRe, 2025](https://proceedings.iclr.cc/paper_files/paper/2025/file/871ac99fdc5282d0301934d23945ebaa-Paper-Conference.pdf), training changes model parameters to improve subsequent self-correction. This connects training work to later performance. A correct answer available during training is not thereby available during correction at test time. Preserve that difference when deciding what checking means the operating arrangement can use.

These papers support particular mechanisms and bounded experimental conclusions. E.23:11 explains further agent and harness mechanisms and their evidence limits. Relative advantage, transfer to another task and coverage of current AI practice require the corresponding comparison.

**Choose what must change in the human–AI work.** Start from the result its recipient needs. To diagnose a past attempt, establish what information reached the performer, which action they chose and executed, and how they used its return. To design a check before the first attempt, identify the choice the performer must make, what information will be available then, and what would let the recipient distinguish that choice from following a supplied answer. A compiler diagnostic, a current-state observation, an expert judgement and a preference supply different grounds. Relate each to the question it can settle. A proposed call needs an available interface and authority; its reported success needs the evidence required by the receiving use. Use [SYSE.42](DPFs/Engineering%20DPF%20Suite/SYSTEMS-ENGINEERING-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#syse42---use-a-selected-tool-and-apply-its-result) to bind and perform the operation and apply its qualified return.

Use the available grounds to decide whether a change is needed and what it should affect: the current answer, an input, retained experience, the procedure or assistance rule, model parameters, a person's ability, or the provision of work. These are possible changed objects, not phases which every attempt must traverse. Explain how the chosen change will reach a later action and which observation would justify retaining it. Keep three decisions separate: ending the current search, accepting the current result and retaining a way for future use. A precise report of a missing fact can justify stopping without completing the requested intervention; a correct result on this occasion can suffice without developing a reusable procedure.

**Retained experience needs both content and a way to use it.** [ACE v3, 2026, §§3 and 5](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.04618v3) develops context through generator attempts, reflection and a curator's incremental updates to identifiable entries. Later attempts consume the retained guidance; entries can be revised or removed. Its limits include dependence on feedback and reflection quality, and cases where a simple reusable rule is enough. [Agentic Experience Learning, EMNLP 2026, §3](https://aclanthology.org/2026.findings-emnlp.1316/) instead distinguishes records of episodes from policies for retrieving experience: a fast selection process updates its evidence about available policies, while a slower synthesis can propose new retrieval policies. Its reported financial comparison freezes learning before final evaluation. That experiment therefore does not establish the behavior of indefinitely changing deployed memory.

For the receiving work, determine whether the needed record was preserved, retrieved and used. [SYSE.43](DPFs/Engineering%20DPF%20Suite/SYSTEMS-ENGINEERING-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#syse43---maintain-external-memory-for-continuing-work) develops that connection. If a relevant return was dropped from the next input, restore it; if the procedure ignores an available result, change the consuming operation. Adding records or another reflector would address a different object. A fixed rule can update memory or select an action without another controller. When a participant selects and changes the rule itself, recover that participant's observations, criterion, authority and subsequent check before describing the additional control relation.

**Judge an instruction at the decision it is meant to change.** A design commission can finish with a supported revision. The revised rule may leave ordinary cases untouched while changing what a performer should do on an exception. To establish an effect on performance, examine executions under conditions the revision affects. [GEPA v2, 2026, §§3–4](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.19457v2) executes candidate prompts within a retained program, uses traces and task feedback to propose revisions, and compares candidates on their execution. This changes prompts while the underlying model weights stay fixed. Its candidate pool preserves alternatives useful on different cases; the data used to choose among them serve selection, while separate test data support a further-use claim. Apply [C.40:4.7](FPF-Spec.md#c4047---develop-a-way-by-what-its-use-produces) when choosing what to retain from such search. A directly understood procedure repair can be sufficient; a prompt search is useful when its exploration and comparison supply a needed contribution.

**Model training changes another part of the arrangement.** [TSRL, EMNLP 2026, §4](https://aclanthology.org/2026.findings-emnlp.19/) combines reinforcement learning with expert guidance selected for difficult states and changes the weight of that supervision during training. The returned product is an updated policy, whose later performance still needs checking. To construct such a change, [SYSE.45](DPFs/Engineering%20DPF%20Suite/SYSTEMS-ENGINEERING-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#syse45---train-an-llm-policy-from-qualified-interaction-experience) connects qualified interaction experience to the selected parameter update and tests new behavior alongside earlier useful behavior. Changing facts, execution access and permissions remain runtime contributions wherever the task needs them. Without appropriate training access or a qualified target, select another available change or return that missing condition.

**Choose the human contribution by its result.** A person can supply a fact unavailable to the model, clarify an intention, challenge an inference, make an authorized commitment, perform an action, or learn to judge and act. Recover the contribution needed now and the conditions for supplying it. Subject competence and authority to change a promise are different grounds. An approval request is useful only when its recipient can make the required decision; explanation or a corrected factual premise can be the missing contribution instead. [Library.Reference:9.3](#libraryreference93---acquiring-sustaining-and-changing-a-shared-humanai-professional-culture) connects supported performance, development and joint work.

When the intended result includes a person's ability, preserve that person's needed judgement or action and observe it under the intended help. HCD.7 and HCD.9–HCD.13 supply the support, practice and evidence choices. [Shen and Tamkin's 2026 study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.20245v2) makes the distinction consequential: in its short experiment with programmers learning an unfamiliar Python library, the group offered AI assistance scored lower on the subsequent unaided quiz. The main time comparison was inconclusive, and the study did not measure long-term retention. Its exploratory interaction patterns do not establish one generally superior teaching method. A good joint artifact and the learner's acquired contribution therefore remain separate questions.

**Reconsider the surrounding arrangement when its conditions change.** A new model, tool or workload can make an old support unnecessary or reveal a missing one. [Anthropic's March 2026 engineering account](https://www.anthropic.com/research/harness-design-long-running-apps) describes removing some resets and subdivisions as models changed while retaining useful evaluation. Treat it as an experienced design account, not a controlled comparison proving one agent organization best. Use [SYSE.46](DPFs/Engineering%20DPF%20Suite/SYSTEMS-ENGINEERING-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#syse46---test-an-agent-and-its-support-in-representative-work) to compare the actual arrangements at the intended reliance, including their needed checks and full burden.

[Engineering.Reference:19.7](DPFs/Engineering%20DPF%20Suite/ENGINEERING-DPF-SUITE-REFERENCE.md#engineeringreference197---develop-the-arrangement-across-attempts) develops the connected choice from a failed attempt to a supported repair, construction and later use. It distinguishes a missing premise from a defective help decision, a lost input and an ignored return, and compares fixed-model changes with training. Use it when an actual attempt leaves the repair undecided. For a check commissioned before the first run, §19.7.5 shows how to construct two distinguishable outcomes from the available instructions or an executable model and how to leave the choice under examination to the performer. If the current method and its available support already obtain the required result, complete the work with them. Select further development for a specified improvement whose expected value warrants its burden.

##### Library.Reference:6.5.3.7 - KCS: resolving requests and changing the conditions of later resolution

The KCS Solve Loop connects understanding a request, finding and using knowledge, and improving it during resolution. Practices are selected as needed; a new or improved article can arise within the work. Evolve Loop uses patterns in requests and knowledge reuse. Knowledge domain experts, coaches and the KCS Council can improve content standards and workflow, while business owners can address causes of recurring problems. The resulting changes affect later requests and their resolution.

Individual uses and management of their shared conditions are connected; knowledge improvement already occurs within Solve. Actual authority and participation determine the realization. The two loop names do not determine an organizational hierarchy.

**Follow the result into its next use.** A supported service answer lets the requestor continue. Usable shared knowledge lets a later responder recognize the applicable resolution. A developed capability lets the practitioner perform the relevant operation in subsequent work. Changed tools, rights or workflow provide different conditions for that work. One episode can contribute to several results. Follow how each result is used and which observation supports the claim about it.

**Use the observation that can distinguish the needed change.** The [Content Standard Checklist](https://library.serviceinnovation.org/KCS/Knowledge-Centered_Success_Practices_Guide/301-Evolve_Loop/Practice_5_Content_Health/Technique_5.4) examines how sampled articles meet the content standard, such as whether their context and resolution are clear. It is not a technical validation of the answer. [Process Alignment Review (PAR)](https://library.serviceinnovation.org/KCS/Knowledge-Centered_Success_Practices_Guide/301-Evolve_Loop/Practice_6_Process_Integration/Technique_6.3) examines sampled cases: what opportunities existed to reuse, improve or create knowledge, and what the responder did. A link can exist without pointing to an applicable resolution. Bring these observations to the people doing the work, with the case conditions and receiving result. [OPS.22](DPFs/Management%20DPF%20Suite/OPERATIONS-MANAGEMENT-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md#ops22---interpret-operating-performance-and-decide-the-response) helps participants interpret these observations. If a response is needed, they can propose it to the participant authorized to decide or act within their own authority. An activity count alone cannot select training or establish individual merit.

**Connect support to the practitioner's own action.** A coach can examine cases and articles, ask the practitioner to demonstrate their search or correction, give usable feedback and agree the operation to try before the next meeting. They return to that attempt and its conditions to choose correction, further practice or a sufficient stop. [Structuring Coaching Sessions](https://library.serviceinnovation.org/KCS/Coach_Reference_Guide/20_Coaching_Session) connects those encounters and includes feedback on the coach's own work. [HCD.3 and HCD.9](DPFs/Human%20Capability%20Engineering%20DPF%20Suite/HUMAN-CAPABILITY-DEVELOPMENT-PRINCIPLES-FRAMEWORK.md) supply the distinction between a human difficulty and missing conditions, and practice through the learner's own attempt, feedback and any needed retry. [Coaching for Success](https://library.serviceinnovation.org/KCS/Knowledge-Centered_Success_Practices_Guide/301-Evolve_Loop/Practice_6_Process_Integration/Technique_6.5) requires a capable coach, time and a workable relationship with the practitioner and manager. C.40.JD connects that development to the method and its provision. Correcting an article for someone can help the service while leaving their capability unestablished.

**Make rights effective for the work.** The [KCS proficiency model](https://library.serviceinnovation.org/KCS/Knowledge-Centered_Success_Practices_Guide/301-Evolve_Loop/Practice_6_Process_Integration/Technique_6.4) connects demonstrated practice with permissions. One-, two- and three-level arrangements group those rights differently. The levels concern KCS practice and permissions; organizational position and technical expertise are separate.
